Gravidynamics, spinodynamics and electrodynamics within the framework of gravitational quantum field theory
Yue-Liang Wu

Abstract
By noticing the fact that the charged leptons and quarks in the standard model are chirality-based Dirac spinors since their weak interaction violates maximally parity symmetry though they behave as Dirac fermions in electromagnetic interaction, we show that such a chirality-based Dirac spinor possesses not only electric charge gauge symmetry U(1) but also inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry WS(1,3) = SP(1,3) ⋊ \rtimes W 1,3 , which reveals the nature of gravity and spacetime. The gravitational force and spin gauge force are governed by the gauge symmetries W 1 , 3 W^{1,3} and SP(1,3), respectively, and a biframe spacetime with globally flat Minkowski spacetime as base spacetime and locally flat gravigauge spacetime as a fiber is described by the gravigauge field through emergent non-commutative geometry. The gauge-geometry duality and renormalizability in gravitational quantum field theory (GQFT) are carefully discussed. A detailed analysis and systematic investigation on gravidynamics and spinodynamics as well as electrodynamics are carried out within the framework of GQFT. A full discussion on the generalized Dirac equation and Maxwell equation as well as Einstein equation and spin gauge equation is made in biframe spacetime. New effects of gravidynamics as extension of general relativity are particularly analyzed. All dynamic equations of basic fields are demonstrated to preserve the spin gauge covariance and general coordinate covariance due to the spin gauge symmetry and emergent general linear group symmetry GL(1,3,R), so they hold naturally in any spinning reference frame and motional reference frame.



pacs
04.50.+h,11.15.-q,12.20.-m

Keywords
: Inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry, Locally flat gravigauge spacetime, Gravitational relativistic quantum mechanics with generalized Dirac equation, Gravidynamics with generalized Einstein equation, Generalized Maxwell equation in any motional and spinning reference frame

I
Introduction

The theory of electromagnetism was built by Maxwell to unify the electricity and magnetism, which is regarded as the first classical unified field theory. The constancy of the speed of light in Maxwell’s theory inspired Einstein to build up the special theory of relativity (SR)
SR
which unifies space and time into a four dimensional spacetime. Such a spacetime is referred to as four-dimensional Minkowski spacetime which is characterized by the Lorentz symmetry SO(1,3). Later on, Einstein extended the SR to the theory of general relativity (GR)
GR
;
FGR
based on the principle of general coordinate covariance under arbitrary transformations of coordinates, which is characterized by the general linear group symmetry GL(1,3, R). In GR, the gravity is described by the dynamics of Riemannian geometry of curved spacetime instead of globally flat dimensional Minkowski spacetime in SR. The GR indicates that space and time cannot be well defined in such a way that the differences of the spatial coordinates or time coordinates can directly be measured by the standard ways in SR. On the other hand, the relativistic quantum mechanics built by Dirac has successfully combined the quantum mechanics and special relativity, which led to the establishment of quantum field theory (QFT) in globally flat four-dimensional Minkowski spacetime. The framework of QFT was initiated to extend the classical electromagnetism into quantum electrodynamics (QED)
QED1
;
QED2a
;
QED2b
;
QED3a
;
QED3b
;
QED3c
;
QED4a
;
QED4b
, which is governed by the Abelian gauge symmetry U(1). Later on, Yang and Mills extended such an Abelian gauge symmetry to a non-Abelian gauge symmetry SU(2) in describing the isotopic spin gauge invariance
YM
.

The success of the standard model (SM) shows that the basic forces of electroweak and strong interactions are governed by the gauge symmetries U(1)
\times
SU
L
(2)
\times
SU(3)
EW1
;
EW2
;
EW3
;
QCD1
;
QCD2
;
TW
. Namely, the three basic forces in SM can well be described by the dynamics of gauge fields within the framework of quantum field theory formulated in globally flat Minkowski spacetime, which is in contrast to the gravity described by Einstein’s GR built based on the dynamics of Riemannian geometry of curved spacetime. It is such a contradiction that causes an obstacle to make a unified description on the gravity and other three basic forces. Recently, the quantum field theory of gravity was constructed based on the spin and scaling gauge symmetries when treating the gravitational interaction on the same footing as the electroweak and strong interactions, such a gravitational gauge field theory provides a framework of gravitational quantum field theory (GQFT)
GQFT
;
GQFTTK
. The concept of biframe spacetime was shown to play an essential role in GQFT, where a bicovariant vector field defined in biframe spacetime is introduced as a basic gravitational field instead of a metric field in GR to describe the gravitational interaction. In GQFT, the action was proposed to possess a joint symmetry, namely, the Poincaré group symmetry PO(1,3) in Minkowski spacetime of coordinates and a proposed internal Poincaré-type gauge symmetry PG(1,3) in noncoordinate spacetime spanned by the bicovariant vector field. Such a bicovariant vector field was postulated to be a gauge-type field in the coset PG(1,3)/SP(1,3) of internal Poincaré-type gauge symmetry group PG(1,3) with spin gauge group SP(1,3) as a subgroup.

It is clear that the key point is to realize explicitly the group structure of internal Poincaré-type gauge symmetry PG(1,3), which comes to the main purpose in our present paper. Our starting point in this paper is to examine carefully the chiral structure of Dirac fermion. This is because the electron and charged leptons and quarks as basic constituents of matter in SM are actually Weyl fermions although they behave as Dirac fermions in the electromagnetic interaction. The reason is simple by noticing the well-known fact that the weak interactions of leptons and quarks in SM violate maximally the law of parity conservation
PV1
;
PV2
;
VA1
;
VA2
. To make a systematic analysis and detailed investigation, our paper is organized as follows: after a brief introduction in Sect. 1, we will show in Sect. 2 how an inhomogeneous spin symmetry WS(1,3) in Hilbert space is resulted to provide an internal Poincaré-type symmetry. Specifically, by viewing the Dirac fermion in four-dimensional Hilbert space as the superposition of left-handed and right-handed Weyl fermions and representing it into a chirality-based Dirac spinor in a chiral spinor representation of eight dimensional Hilbert space, we are able to demonstrate that such a chirality-based Dirac spinor gets an enlarged inhomogeneous spin symmetry group WS(1,3) = SP(1,3)
\rtimes
W
1,3
, which is a semi-direct product group with SP(1,3) being the spin group and W
1,3
referring to as
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin group. Such a
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin group W
1,3
is shown to be a translation-like chiral-type spin group in eight dimensional Hilbert space. Meanwhile, an internal Abelian symmetry group U(1) remains as electric charge symmetry group. A free motional chirality-based Dirac spinor is verified to possess a maximal associated symmetry when combining the inhomogeneous spin symmetry WS(1,3) in eight dimensional Hilbert space with the Poincaré group symmetry PO(1,3) in four-dimensional Minkowski spacetime of coordinates. In Sect. 3, the inhomogeneous spin symmetry WS(1,3) and electric charge symmetry U(1) are gauged to be local symmetries based on the gauge invariance principle. It is found that the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field brings on the genesis of gravigauge field, which reveals the nature of gravity and brings on the gravitational origin of spin gauge symmetry. The group structure of internal Poincaré-type gauge symmetry PG(1,3) is explicitly realized to be as the inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry, i.e., PG(1,3)
\equiv
WS(1,3) = SP(1,3)
\rtimes
W
1,3
. We further present in Sect. 4 a general demonstration that the non-coordinate spacetime spanned by the gravigauge field basis generates a locally flat gravigauge spacetime characterized by emergent non-commutative geometry. A fiber bundle structure is analyzed in light of biframe spacetime with globally flat Minkowski spacetime as base spacetime and locally flat gravigauge spacetime as a fiber. In Sect. 5, we will show the scaling property of basic fields and make a general definition on gauge fields and field strengths as vector and tensor fields in locally flat gravigauge spacetime. We are going to formulate in Sect. 6 the various formalisms of gauge and scaling invariant actions for the chirality-based Dirac spinor based on the action principle of path integral formulation, which enables us to prove the gauge-gravity and gravity-geometry correspondences and corroborate a gauge-geometry duality relation within the framework of gravitational quantum field theory. A generalized Dirac equation is derived in Sect. 7 to describe the gravitational relativistic quantum theory. Meanwhile, a gravigauge Dirac equation is obtained in locally flat gravigauge spacetime. In Sect. 8, we will present a detailed investigation on the gravidynamics by deriving the gauge-type gravitational equations of gravigauge field in locally flat gravigauge spacetime and biframe spacetime. A geometric gravidynamics is discussed as the extension of general relativity and also analyzed in locally flat gravigauge spacetime. We provide a particular investigation on the new effects of gravidynamics beyond the Einstein theory of general relativity. The spinodynamics is studied in Sect. 9 by deriving the equation of motion for the spin gauge field. In Sect. 10, we provide a systematic analysis on the electrodynamics in the presence of gravitational interaction. Various generalized Maxwell equations are derived to characterize the so-called gravigeometry-medium electrodynamics in the presence of gravimetric field and the gravigauge-mediated electrodynamics obtained in locally flat gravigauge spacetime. Meanwhile, the electromagnetic-like gravimetric-gauge field is introduced in generalized Maxwell equations to describe the gravimetric-gauge-mediated electrodynamics formulated in dynamic Riemannian spacetime. We will further show in Sect. 11 that the dynamics of all basic fields obeys the general coordinate covariance in curved Riemannian spacetime and the spin gauge covariance in locally flat gravigauge spacetime. it is demonstrated that the gravigeometry-medium electrodynamics holds in any motional reference frame and the gravigauge-mediated electrodynamics is applicable in any spinning reference frame. In particular, the gravigeometry-medium Maxwell equations in special background medium are explicitly demonstrated to maintain the general coordinate covariance in motional reference frame, and the gravigauge Maxwell equations in special background medium are examined to keep the spin gauge covariance. Our conclusions and discussions are presented in the final section.

II
Inhomogeneous spin symmetry WS(1,3) and maximal associated symmetry for chirality-based Dirac spinor

The basic constituents of nature are known to be the leptons and quarks as indicated in SM. The observed matter consists of the electrons and nucleons which are made of the up and down quarks, they all appear as Dirac fermions in the electromagnetic interaction. Their free motion is described by the following relativistic quantum Dirac equation
DE
:

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{E}-c\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\mathsf{\bf{p}}-\beta mc^{2}\right)\psi=0,\mbox{or}

\displaystyle\left(\mathsf{E}+c\boldsymbol{\alpha}\cdot\mathsf{\bf{p}}+\beta mc^{2}\right)\psi=0,

with
\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
and
\beta
the
4\times 4
Hermitian matrices satisfying the conditions
\alpha_{i}\beta=-\beta\alpha_{i}
,
\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}=-\alpha_{j}\alpha_{i}
(
i\neq j
) and
\alpha_{i}^{2}=\beta^{2}=1
.
\psi
is the Dirac fermion with a complex four-component entity
\psi^{T}=(\psi_{1}\,,\psi_{2}\,,\psi_{3}\,,\psi_{4})
in the spinor representation of four-dimensional Hilbert space. A general covariant formulation of the Dirac equation can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\gamma^{\mu}i\partial_{\mu}-m\right)\psi=0,

with
\mu=0,1,2,3
,
\gamma^{0}=\beta
and
\gamma^{i}=\beta\alpha^{i}
. Where
\partial_{\mu}=\partial/\partial x^{\mu}
is the 4-dimensional coordinate derivative and
\gamma_{\mu}
the 4
\times
4
\gamma
-matrices which satisfy the anticommutation relations,

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{\mu}\,\,\gamma_{\nu}\}=\eta_{\mu\nu},\qquad\eta_{\mu\nu}=\mathop{\rm diag}.(1,-1,-1,-1).

The hermitian action in obtaining the above Dirac equation can be constructed as follows:

\displaystyle S_{D}=\int d^{4}x\,\frac{1}{2}\left(\bar{\psi}(x)\gamma^{\mu}i\partial_{\mu}\psi(x)+H.c.\right)-m\,\bar{\psi}(x)\psi(x),

(1)

with
\bar{\psi}(x)=\psi^{\dagger}(x)\gamma^{0}
and
m
being the mass of Dirac fermion.

In the SM of electroweak and strong interactions, the charged leptons and quarks are actually Weyl fermions due to their weak interactions which bring on the maximal parity violation. Such a Dirac fermion in the electromagnetic interaction should be regarded as the superposition of left-handed and right-handed Weyl fermions. By representing the Dirac fermion in a chiral spinor representation of eight-dimensional Hilbert space, we can express the action of free motion massive Dirac fermion given in Eq.(
1
) into the following equivalent form:

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x\,\frac{1}{2}\{\bar{\Psi}_{-}(x)\Gamma^{a}\Gamma_{-}\delta_{a}^{\;\;\mu}iD_{\mu}\Psi_{-}(x)-\bar{\Psi}_{-}(x)(m_{5}\Gamma^{5}\Gamma_{-}+m_{6}\Gamma^{6}\Gamma_{-})\Psi_{-}(x)+H.c.\},

(2)

where we have introduced the following definitions:

\displaystyle\Psi_{-}(x)=\Gamma_{-}\Psi(x)\equiv\binom{\psi_{L}(x)}{\psi_{R}(x)},

\displaystyle\psi_{L,R}(x)=\gamma_{\mp}\psi(x),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mp}=\frac{1}{2}(1\mp\hat{\gamma}_{7}),\quad\gamma_{\mp}=\frac{1}{2}(1\mp\gamma_{5}),

\displaystyle\Gamma^{a}=\sigma_{0}\otimes\gamma^{a},\quad\gamma^{a}=\delta^{a}_{\;\mu}\gamma^{\mu},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{5}=i\sigma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{5},\quad\Gamma^{6}=i\sigma_{2}\otimes\gamma_{5},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\hat{a}}\equiv 2(\Sigma_{-}^{\hat{a}}+\Sigma_{+}^{\hat{a}}),\quad\Sigma_{\mp}^{\hat{a}}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{\hat{a}}\Gamma_{\mp},

\displaystyle\gamma_{5}=-i\gamma^{0}\gamma^{1}\gamma^{2}\gamma^{3}=\sigma_{3}\otimes\sigma_{0},

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{7}=-\Gamma^{0}\Gamma^{1}\Gamma^{2}\Gamma^{3}\Gamma^{5}\Gamma^{6}=\sigma_{3}\otimes\gamma_{5},

(3)

with
\hat{a}\equiv(a,6,7)
. Where
\gamma^{a}
and
\gamma_{5}
are the usual
\gamma
matrices and
\delta_{a}^{\;\;\mu}
is the Kronecker symbol. Note that
\gamma_{\mp}
and
\Gamma_{-}
provide the chiral project operators via the chiral
\gamma
-matrices
\gamma_{5}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{7}
defined in four-dimensional and eight-dimensional Hilbert spaces, respectively.

It is noticed that
\Psi_{-}(x)
defines a chirality-based Dirac spinor in the chiral spinor representation of eight-dimensional Hilbert space with
\psi_{L,R}(x)
denoting the ordinary left-handed and right-handed Weyl fermions in four-dimensional Hilbert space. Note that we have introduced the Greek alphabet (
\mu,\nu=0,1,2,3
) and Latin alphabet (
a,b,=0,1,2,3
) to distinguish four-vector indices in coordinate spacetime and noncoordinate spacetime, respectively. Both the Greek and Latin indices are raised and lowered by the constant metric matrices, i.e.,
\eta^{\mu\nu}
and
\eta^{ab}
with
\eta^{\mu\nu}
(
\eta_{\mu\nu}
)=
\mathop{\rm diag}.
(1,-1,-1,-1) and
\eta^{ab}
(
\eta_{ab}
)=
\mathop{\rm diag}.
(1,-1,-1,-1). All the scalar product of vectors and tensors is obtained via the contraction with the constant metric matrices
\eta^{\mu\nu}
and
\eta^{ab}
, i.e.,
V^{\mu}V_{\mu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}V_{\mu}V_{\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}V^{\mu}V^{\nu}
and
A^{a}A_{a}=\eta^{ab}A_{a}A_{b}=\eta_{ab}A^{a}A^{b}
. The system of units is chosen such that
c=\hbar=1
.

The mass term of Dirac fermion is produced via the following relations:

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{-}(x)(m_{5}\Gamma^{5}\Gamma_{-}+m_{6}\Gamma^{6}\Gamma_{-})\Psi_{-}(x)=m\bar{\Psi}_{-}(x)\Gamma^{6}\Gamma_{-}e^{i2\alpha_{p}\gamma_{7}}\Psi_{-}(x)

\displaystyle\equiv m\bar{\Psi}_{-}(x)\Gamma^{6}\Gamma_{-}e^{-i2\alpha_{p}\gamma_{5}}\Psi_{-}(x)\to m\bar{\Psi}_{-}(x)\Gamma^{6}\Gamma_{-}\Psi_{-}(x)=m\bar{\psi}\psi,

(4)

where we have made a replacement in obtaining the last form:

\displaystyle\Psi_{-}(x)\to e^{-i\alpha_{p}\gamma_{7}}\Psi_{-}(x)\equiv e^{i\alpha_{p}\gamma_{5}}\Psi_{-}(x),

(5)

with

\displaystyle\gamma_{7}=\sigma_{3}\otimes\sigma_{0}\otimes\sigma_{0},\quad\tan 2\alpha_{p}=m_{5}/m_{6},

\displaystyle m=\sqrt{m_{5}^{2}+m_{6}^{2}}.

(6)

Note that such a replacement for the chirality-based Dirac spinor preserves the kinetic term of the action in Eq.(
2
) due to its invariance under such a transformation.

The
\Gamma
-matrices
\Gamma^{\hat{a}}
with
\hat{a}=(a,5,6)
and
\hat{\gamma}_{7}
defined in eight-dimensional Hilbert space satisfy the anticommuting relations of Clifford algebra:

\displaystyle\{\Gamma^{\hat{a}},\Gamma^{\hat{b}}\}=\eta^{\hat{a}\hat{b}},\quad\{\Gamma^{\hat{a}},\hat{\gamma}_{7}\}=0,

(7)

which indicates that the mass of Dirac fermion may have a geometric origin via the extra dimension of spacetime as discussed in ref.
GQFT6D
.

It can be verified that the action given in Eq.(
2
) possesses the following associated symmetry:

\displaystyle G_{S}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P^{1,3}\ltimes SO(1,3)\,\widetilde{\Join}\,SP(1,3)\rtimes W^{1,3}\times U(1)

(8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle PO(1,3)\,\widetilde{\Join}\,WS(1,3)\times U(1),

where the symbol “
\,\widetilde{\Join}\,
” is adopted to notate the associated symmetry in which the transformation of spin symmetry group SP(1,3) in Hilbert space must be coincidental to that of the isomorphic Lorentz symmetry group SO(1,3) in Minkowski spacetime.

PO(1,3) denotes the inhomogeneous Lorentz symmetry group or Poincaré symmetry group in four-dimensional Minkowski spacetime,

\displaystyle PO(1,3)=P^{1,3}\ltimes SO(1,3),

where the symbol ‘
\ltimes
’ is used to indicate that PO(1,3) is a semi-direct product group with P
1,3
representing the translational symmetry group of coordinates in Minkowski spacetime.

WS(1,3) is an enlarged spin symmetry group, which is also a semi-direct product group expressed as follows:

\displaystyle WS(1,3)\equiv SP(1,3)\rtimes W^{1,3}.

(9)

It can be checked that SP(1,3) group generators
\Sigma^{ab}
and W
1,3
group generators
\Sigma_{-}^{a}
have the following explicit forms:

\displaystyle\Sigma^{ab}=\frac{i}{4}[\Gamma^{a},\Gamma^{b}],

\displaystyle\Sigma_{-}^{a}=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{a}\Gamma_{-},\quad\Gamma_{-}=\frac{1}{2}(1-\hat{\gamma}_{7}),

(10)

which satisfy the group algebra:

\displaystyle[\Sigma^{ab},\Sigma^{cd}]=i(\Sigma^{ad}\eta^{bc}-\Sigma^{bd}\eta^{ac}-\Sigma^{ac}\eta^{bd}+\Sigma^{bc}\eta^{ad}),

\displaystyle[\Sigma^{ab},\Sigma_{-}^{c}]=i(\Sigma_{-}^{a}\eta^{bc}-\Sigma_{-}^{b}\eta^{ac}),

\displaystyle[\Sigma_{-}^{a},\Sigma_{-}^{b}]=0,

(11)

where
W^{1,3}
appears as a translation-like Abelian-type symmetry group in Hilbert space.

Let us now discuss explicitly the translation-like Abelian-type symmetry group
W^{1,3}
. The chirality-based Dirac spinor
\Psi_{-}(x)
transforms under
W^{1,3}
group operation as follows:

\displaystyle\Psi_{-}(x)\to\Psi^{\prime}_{-}(x)=S(\varpi)\Psi_{-}(x),

\displaystyle S(\varpi)=e^{i\varpi_{a}\Sigma_{-}^{a}/2}=1+i\varpi_{a}\Sigma_{-}^{a}/2,

(12)

which can be rewritten into the following forms:

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime}_{-}(x)\equiv\Psi_{-}^{(w)}(x)+\tilde{\Psi}_{-}^{(e)}(x),

\displaystyle\Psi_{-}^{(w)}(x)\equiv\Psi_{-}(x),\quad\tilde{\Psi}_{-}^{(e)}(x)\equiv i\frac{1}{2}\varpi_{a}\Sigma_{-}^{a}\Psi_{-}(x),

(13)

where
\Psi_{-}^{(w)}(x)
and
\tilde{\Psi}_{-}^{(e)}(x)
have opposite chiral property under the operation of chiral operator
\hat{\gamma}_{7}
in eight-dimensional Hilbert space, i.e.:

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}_{7}\Psi_{-}^{(w)}(x)=-\Psi_{-}^{(w)}(x),\quad\hat{\gamma}_{7}\tilde{\Psi}_{-}^{(e)}(x)=+\tilde{\Psi}_{-}^{(e)}(x).

(14)

For that,
\Psi_{-}^{(w)}(x)
and
\tilde{\Psi}_{-}^{(e)}(x)
are referred to as westward and eastward chirality-based Dirac spinors indicated by the upper indices
``w"
and
``e"
, respectively, in order to distinguish with the left-handed and right-handed Weyl fermions
\psi_{L}(x)
and
\psi_{R}(x)
defined in four-dimensional Hilbert space.

As such an Abelian-type spin symmetry group W
1,3
is associated with the sign flip in chirality of westward and eastward spinor fields for the initial and shifted new parts, we may refer to the translation-like chiral-type symmetry W
1,3
as
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin symmetry
for short. So we may refer to the enlarged spin symmetry group WS(1,3) = SP(1,3)
\rtimes
W
1,3
as
inhomogeneous spin symmetry group
acting on the chirality-based Dirac spinor
\Psi_{-}(x)
, which is a semi-direct product group as indicated by the symbol ‘
\rtimes
’. It is intriguing to notice that such an inhomogeneous spin symmetry group is in correspondence to the inhomogeneous Lorentz symmetry group (or Poincaré symmetry group) PO(1,3) = P
{}^{1,3}\ltimes
SO(1,3).

It is essential to distinguish the spin symmetry SP(1,3) with the Lorentz symmetry SO(1,3) as they operate on different spaces. The former acts on the chirality-based Dirac spinor field
\Psi_{-}(x)
in Hilbert space and the latter operates on the coordinates in Minkowski spacetime. They become the associated symmetry just for the free motion Dirac fermion in globally flat Minkowski spacetime.

It is easy to check that the action in Eq.(
2
) possesses an Abelian group symmetry U(1) under the following transformation:

\displaystyle\Psi_{-}(x)\to\Psi^{\prime}_{-}(x)=e^{i\varpi}\Psi_{-}(x),

(15)

which describes the electric charge symmetry.

It is clear that the symmetry shown in Eq.(
8
) presents the maximal associated symmetry for a free motion Dirac fermion expressed in the chiral spinor representation of eight-dimensional Hilbert space.

III
Inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry with the genesis of gravigauge field and the gravitational origin of spin gauge symmetry

It is the essential point to distinguish the group symmetries in Hilbert space and Minkowski spacetime when proposing the gauge invariance principle to study the fundamental interaction. The gauge invariance principle states that
the laws of nature should be independent of the choice of local field configurations
, from which the inhomogeneous spin symmetry WS(1,3) and electric charge symmetry U(1) of chirality-based Dirac spinor
\Psi_{-}(x)
are postulated to be local gauge symmetries. Meanwhile, the inhomogeneous Lorentz group symmetry (Poincaré group symmetry) PO(1,3) of coordinates is considered to remain a global symmetry. In such a consideration, the inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry WS(1, 3) in Hilbert space of spinor field does become distinguishable from the inhomogeneous Lorentz group symmetry PO(1,3) in Minkowski spacetime of coordinates. It becomes natural that the inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry group WS(1,3) provides the so-called internal Poincaré-type gauge symmetry group PG(1,3) proposed in
GQFT
, i.e., PG(1,3)
\equiv
WS(1,3) = SP(1,3)
\rtimes
W
1,3
.

III.1
Inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry with the genesis of gravigauge field

Based on the gauge invariance principle, the gauge fields
\hat{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)
and
A_{\mu}(x)
in correspondence to the inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry WS(1, 3) and electromagnetic gauge symmetry U(1) are introduced to ensure the theory be gauge invariance. To realize such a gauge invariant theory, it is practically carried out by replacing the usual derivative operator of coordinates in Minkowski spacetime into the following covariant derivative operator:

\displaystyle i\partial_{\mu}\to i\hat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu}\equiv i\partial_{\mu}+\hat{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)+A_{\mu}(x),

(16)

with

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)\equiv{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)+\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)\equiv{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{\;ab}(x)\,\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{ab},\quad\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)\equiv{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{-a},

(17)

where
\hat{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)
is referred to as inhomogeneous spin gauge field with
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{\;ab}(x)
representing the spin gauge field relevant to the spin gauge symmetry SP(1, 3) and
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
denoting the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge field relating to the translation-like
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin Abelian gauge symmetry
W^{1,3}
.
A_{\mu}(x)
is the electromagnetic gauge field corresponding to the U(1) gauge symmetry.

From the general covariant derivative
\hat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mathbf{M}}
, the field strength of gauge field
\hat{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mathbf{M}}(x)
is obtained as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\nu}\equiv i[\hat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\mu},\hat{{\mathcal{D}}}_{\nu}]={\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}+\check{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\nu}+F_{\mu\nu},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}\equiv{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{ab}=\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{A}}_{\nu}(x)-\partial_{\nu}{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)-i[{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x),{\mathcal{A}}_{\nu}(x)],

\displaystyle\check{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\nu}\equiv{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{-a}={\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\nu}(x)-{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad\equiv\partial_{\mu}\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\nu}(x)-\partial_{\nu}\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)-i({\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\nu}(x)-{\mathcal{A}}_{\nu}(x)\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)),

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}\equiv F_{\mu\nu}(x)=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}(x)-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}(x),

(18)

It can be checked that the gauge transformation properties of gauge fields can explicitly be given as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)\to{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)=S(\Lambda){\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)S^{-1}(\Lambda)+S(\Lambda)i\partial_{\mu}S^{-1}(\Lambda),

\displaystyle\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)\to\check{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)=S(\Lambda)\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)S^{-1}(\Lambda),\quad S(\Lambda)\in SP(1,3),

(19)

or

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(x)=\Lambda^{a}_{\;c}(x)\Lambda^{b}_{\;d}(x){\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{cd}(x)+\frac{1}{2}(\Lambda^{a}_{\;c}(x)\partial_{\mu}\Lambda^{bc}(x)-\Lambda^{b}_{\;c}(x)\partial_{\mu}\Lambda^{ac}(x)),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=\Lambda^{a}_{\;c}(x){\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{c}(x),\quad\Lambda^{a}_{\;c}(x)\in SP(1,3)\cong SO(1,3),

(20)

under the gauge transformation of SP(1,3) gauge symmetry, and

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\to{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)={\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{a}(x)+{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varpi^{a}(x),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varpi^{a}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\varpi^{a}(x)+{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu\,b}^{a}(x)\varpi^{b}(x),

(21)

under the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge transformation of W
1,3
gauge symmetry.

It is noticed that the presence of gauge symmetry in the action involves actually redundant degrees of freedom. To eliminate the redundant degrees of freedom arising from the gauge symmetry, it needs to make a gauge prescription by imposing a gauge fixing condition. In order to extract the redundant degrees of freedom, we are initiated to decompose the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge field
\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
into the following two parts:

\displaystyle\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)\equiv{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{-a}=\left(\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)+\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\right)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{-a},

(22)

where
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
is supposed to get an inhomogeneous gauge transformation and
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
becomes gauge invariant under the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge transformation. Meanwhile, they should transform as covariant vector fields under the gauge transformation of spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3). The explicit transformations are presented as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\to\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)+{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varpi^{a}(x),

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\to\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x),

(23)

under the gauge transformation of
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge symmetry W
1,3
, and

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\to\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=\Lambda^{a}_{\;\;b}(x)\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{b}(x),

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\to\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=\Lambda^{a}_{\;\;b}(x)\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{b}(x),

(24)

under the gauge transformation of spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3).

We may refer
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
to be as a
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin non-homogeneous gauge field and
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
as a
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field. From the property of
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge transformation, it is natural to express
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
into the following form:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\equiv{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varkappa^{a}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\varkappa^{a}(x)+{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu b}^{a}(x)\varkappa^{b}(x),

(25)

where we have introduced a vector field
\varkappa^{a}(x)
in Hilbert space. Correspondingly, the field strength of
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge field can also be decomposed into two parts:

\displaystyle\check{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\equiv\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)+{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x),

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)={\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\mathbf{\Omega}_{\nu}^{a}(x)-{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{a}(x)={\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\varkappa_{b}(x),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)={\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{a}(x)-{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;\equiv\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{a}(x)-\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)+{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu\,b}^{a}(x)\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{b}(x)-{\mathcal{A}}_{\nu\,b}^{a}(x)\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{b}(x).

(26)

To maintain the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge transformation, the vector field
\varkappa^{a}(x)
transforms as follows:

\displaystyle\varkappa^{a}(x)\to\varkappa^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=\varkappa^{a}(x)+\varpi^{a}(x).

(27)

which behaves as a translation of vector field
\varkappa^{a}(x)
in non-coordinate field spacetime. As such a transformation reflects the symmetry property of translation-like
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin Abelian-type gauge group W
1,3
, we may mention
\varkappa^{a}(x)
as
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin vector field. It is seen that under the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge transformation,
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)
becomes
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field strength and the field strength
\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)
transforms as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\to\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)+{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\varpi_{b}(x).

(28)

It indicates that to remove the degrees of freedom, we are able to take an appropriate
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge transformation to make a gauge fixing condition, so that the following relation holds, i.e.:

\displaystyle\varkappa^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=\varkappa^{a}(x)+\varpi^{a}(x)=0,\quad\mbox{i.e.},\quad\varpi^{a}(x)=-\varkappa^{a}(x),

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=0,\quad\mathcal{R}_{\mu\nu}^{{}^{\prime}a}(x)=0.

(29)

Therefore, by choosing the gauge fixing condition, we can always take the gauge field and field strength to be as follows:

\displaystyle\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x)\equiv{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{-a}=\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{-a},

\displaystyle\check{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv\check{{\mathcal{F}}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{-a}\equiv{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{-a}.

(30)

III.2
Gravitational origin of spin gauge symmetry

In analogous, to clarify the redundant degrees of freedom from the spin gauge symmetry, we are motivated to decompose the spin gauge field
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)
into the following two parts:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x)=\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}(x)+\mathbf{A}_{\mu}(x)\equiv\left(\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)+\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\right)\frac{1}{2}\varSigma_{ab},

(31)

where
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}(x)
obeys inhomogeneous gauge transformation of the spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3) and
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}(x)
transforms homogeneously under the spin gauge transformation, i.e.:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}(x)\to\mathbf{\Omega}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)=S(\Lambda)i\partial_{\mu}S^{-1}(\Lambda)+S(\Lambda)\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}(x)S^{-1}(\Lambda),

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mu}(x)\to\mathbf{A}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x)=S(\Lambda)\mathbf{A}_{\mu}(x)S^{-1}(\Lambda),\quad S(\Lambda)\in SP(1,3).

(32)

Unlike the usual internal gauge field for which the inhomogeneous gauge transformation part is thought to be characterized by a pure gauge field with a vanishing field strength,
the gauge field
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}
under the inhomogeneous gauge transformation is considered to be relevant to the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}
in order to maintain the independent degrees of freedom arising from the inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry WS(1,3)
. Namely, the total numbers of independent degrees of freedom for the gauge field should be fixed to be the same as the initial ones required from the inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry WS(1,3) which brings about both the spin gauge field
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
.

Indeed, it can be verified that
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}
is completely determined through the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}
with the following explicit form:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{a\nu}\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{b}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{b\nu}\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{a\rho}\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{b\sigma}\mathsf{F}_{\rho\sigma}^{c}\mathbf{A}_{\mu c}\right),

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{a}(x)-\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x),

(33)

where we have introduced the dual
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{a}(x)=\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)\mathbf{A}_{\nu b}(x)\eta^{ab}=\eta_{\nu}^{\;\;\mu},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{b}(x)=\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)\mathbf{A}^{\nu b}(x)\eta_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{a}^{\;\;b},

(34)

which indicates that when regarding
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
as a matrix field,
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
is viewed as the inverse matrix field of
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
. The existence of
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
requires a non-zero determinant of matrix field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}(x)\equiv\det\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x)\neq 0,\quad\mbox{or}\quad\hat{\mathfrak{A}}(x)\equiv\det\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\mu}(x)\neq 0.

(35)

Note that the field strength
\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)
is
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge invariant but no longer spin gauge covariant, which distinguishes, as indicated by a different letter style, from the spin gauge covariant field strength
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)
defined in Eq.(
III.1
).

It can be checked that the gauge transformation of
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}
in the vector representation of spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3) does bring
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}
to a proper gauge transformation in the adjoint representation of spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3), i.e.:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}\;a}(x)=\Lambda^{a}_{\;\;c}(x)\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;c}(x),\quad\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{{}^{\prime}\;\mu}(x)=\Lambda_{a}^{\;\;c}(x)\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{c}^{\;\mu}(x),\quad\Lambda^{a}_{\;c}(x)\in\mbox{SP(1,3)},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(x)=\Lambda^{a}_{\;c}(x)\Lambda^{b}_{\;d}(x)\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{cd}(x)+\frac{1}{2}(\Lambda^{a}_{\;c}(x)\partial_{\mu}\Lambda^{bc}(x)-\Lambda^{b}_{\;c}(x)\partial_{\mu}\Lambda^{ac}(x)).

(36)

In general, there is no way to eliminate the spin gauge field part
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
by making a spin gauge transformation, which differs completely from the usual internal gauge field. This can be verified from the spin gauge field strength which can also be decomposed into two parts:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)+{\mathbf{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x),

with the following explicit forms:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{\Omega}_{\nu}^{ab}-\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}+\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu c}^{a}\mathbf{\Omega}_{\nu}^{cb}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{\nu c}^{a}\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{cb},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)=\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{ab}-\mathbf{D}_{\nu}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}+\mathbf{A}_{\mu c}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{cb}-\mathbf{A}_{\nu c}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{cb},

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\mu}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{ab}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{ab}+\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu c}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{cb}+\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu c}^{b}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{ac},

(38)

where
\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}
is purely the field strength of
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
. Since
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
is uniquely determined by the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}
, there are no additional independent degrees of freedom involved in the decomposition of spin gauge field
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
.

It can be verified that the field strength
\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}
is related to Riemann-type curvature tensor as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)=\mathbf{A}_{\rho}^{\;a}(x)\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{b\sigma}(x)\mathbf{R}^{\;\rho}_{\mu\nu\sigma}(x)

(39)

with the Riemann-type curvature tensor
\mathbf{R}^{\rho}_{\mu\nu\sigma}(x)
given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu\sigma}^{\;\rho}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\nu\sigma}^{\rho}-\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\mu\sigma}^{\rho}+\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\mu\lambda}^{\rho}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\nu\sigma}^{\lambda}-\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\nu\lambda}^{\rho}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\mu\sigma}^{\lambda}.

(40)

Where
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\mu\sigma}^{\rho}(x)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\mu\sigma}^{\rho}(x)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\rho}\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{A}_{\sigma}^{\;\;a}+\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\rho}\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu\mspace{1mu}b}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{\sigma}^{\;\;b},

(41)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\hat{\mathsf{H}}^{\rho\lambda}(\partial_{\mu}\mathsf{H}_{\lambda\sigma}+\partial_{\sigma}\mathsf{H}_{\lambda\mu}-\partial_{\lambda}\mathsf{H}_{\mu\sigma})=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\sigma\mu}^{\rho}.

In the second equality we have introduced the gauge invariant dual tensors,

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{\;b}(x)\eta_{ab}\equiv-\mathop{\rm Tr}\nolimits\Gamma^{6}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}\Sigma_{a-}\Gamma^{6}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{\;b}\Sigma_{b-},

\displaystyle\hat{\mathsf{H}}^{\mu\rho}(x)\mathsf{H}_{\rho\nu}(x)=\eta^{\mu}_{\;\;\nu},\quad\hat{\mathsf{H}}^{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\mu}(x)\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{b}^{\;\nu}(x)\eta^{ab}.

(42)

Geometrically, we will show later on that the tensor
\mathsf{H}_{\mu\nu}(x)
(
\hat{\mathsf{H}}^{\mu\nu}(x)
) composed of
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
(
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\mu}(x)
) represents a metric-like tensor which characterizes the gravitational interaction in Einstein’s general theory of relativity. It is clear that the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
should be the basic gauge field which brings the gravitational interaction as fundamental gauge interaction. For convenience of mention,
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
is referred to as
gravigauge field
. Correspondingly,
\mathsf{H}_{\mu\nu}(x)
is referred to as
gravimetric field
. Meanwhile,
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
is referred to as
spin gravigauge field
and
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
as
spin covariant-gauge field
.

It is obvious that the decomposition of spin gauge field
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
into the sum of spin gravigauge field
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and spin covariant-gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
exhibits the gravitational origin of gauge symmetry, which allows us to bring about the corresponding gauge covariant field strengths
\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)
and
{\mathbf{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)
. The spin gravigauge field
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and its gauge covariant field strength
\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)
describe the gravitational interaction along with the genesis of gravigauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
, which realizes the gauge-gravity and gravity-geometry correspondences.

In conclusion, the inhomogeneous spin gauge symmetry WS(1,3) brings on the introduction of spin gauge field
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
via appropriate gauge fixing condition. Meanwhile, the gravitational origin of spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3) for chirality-based Dirac fermion also allows us to choose alternatively the spin covariant-gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
as independent degrees of freedom, where the spin gravigauge field
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
that reflects the non-homogeneous spin gauge symmetry is essentially governed by the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
. Such a
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
as gravigauge field transforms as a bicovariant vector field under the transformations of spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3) and Lorentz group symmetry SO(1,3). Later on, we shall discuss in detail how the general linear group symmetry GL(1,3,R) emerges automatically as the generalization of scaling and gauge invariance principle and brings on the gauge-geometry duality.

IV
Locally flat gravigauge spacetime with emergence of non-commutative geometry and biframe spacetime with fiber bundle structure

The
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
as gravigauge field reveals the nature of gravity as a gauge interaction. The
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin vector field
\varkappa^{\mathbf{A}}(x)
introduced to characterize the translation-like
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge symmetry is regarded as a dimensionless
gravivector field
in a non-coordinate field spacetime. In analogous to the ordinary translation operator
\partial_{\mu}\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}
of coordinates in globally flat Minkowski spacetime, the
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin gauge symmetry as a local translation of gravivector field may be represented via a field translation operator defined in formal by the following coordinate-like field derivative:

\displaystyle\hat{\eth}_{a}\equiv\frac{\delta}{\delta\varkappa^{a}},

(43)

Similarly, we should introduce a coordinate-like field displacement
\delta\varkappa^{\mathbf{A}}
in correspondence to the ordinary coordinate displacement
dx^{\mu}
.

The
{\mathcal{W}}_{e}
-spin invariant-gauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
as gravigauge field is regarded as a bi-covariant vector field under the transformations of spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3) in Hilbert space and global Lorentz symmetry SO(1,3) in coordinate spacetime. It is natural to propose the displacement correspondence that the coordinate-like field displacement
\delta\varkappa^{a}
is directly associated to the ordinary coordinate displacement
dx^{\mu}
via the bi-covariant vector field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
. To be explicit, such a displacement correspondence is presented by the following relations:

\displaystyle\delta\varkappa^{a}\equiv\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)dx^{\mu},\qquad\hat{\eth}_{a}\equiv\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)\partial_{\mu},

(44)

where we may refer to the displacement
\delta\varkappa^{a}
as
dimensionless gravicoordinate displacement
and the derivative
\hat{\eth}_{a}
as
dimensionless gravicoordinate derivative
.

It is useful to introduce the corresponding dimensionful gravicoordinate displacement and derivative. For that, let us express the gravigauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
and its dual field
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
into the following forms:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)\equiv\phi(x)\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x),\quad\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)\equiv\phi^{-1}(x)\hat{\chi}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x),

(45)

where
\phi(x)
is considered as a scalar field with unit canonical dimension, which is referred to as
graviscalar field
.
\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
provides a dimensionless gravigauge field with
\hat{\chi}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
as a dimensionless dual gravigauge field, they satisfy the following conditions:

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)\chi_{\nu}^{\;b}(x)\eta_{ab},\quad\hat{\chi}^{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv\hat{\chi}_{a}^{\;\mu}(x)\hat{\chi}_{b}^{\;\nu}(x)\eta^{ab},

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu\rho}(x)\hat{\chi}^{\rho\nu}(x)=\eta_{\mu}^{\;\;\nu},\quad\hat{\chi}^{\mu\rho}(x)\chi_{\rho\nu}(x)=\eta^{\mu}_{\;\;\nu},

(46)

which enables us to define the
dimensionful gravicoordinate displacement and derivative
as follows:

\displaystyle\delta\zeta^{a}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)dx^{\mu}\equiv\phi^{-1}(x)\delta\varkappa^{a},

\displaystyle\eth_{a}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta\zeta^{a}}=\hat{\chi}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)\partial_{\mu}=\phi(x)\hat{\eth}_{a},

(47)

where the dimensionless gravicoordinate displacement
\delta\varkappa^{a}
and derivative
\hat{\eth}_{a}
with respect to gravivector field
\varkappa^{a}
are related to the corresponding dimensionful gravicoordinate displacement
\delta\zeta^{a}
and derivative
\eth_{a}
via the graviscalar field
\phi(x)
.

Mathematically, it is known that the ordinary derivative vector operator
\partial_{\mu}\equiv\partial/\partial x^{\mu}
at point
x
of Minkowski spacetime
M_{4}
defines a tangent basis
\{\partial_{\mu}\}\equiv\{\partial/\partial x^{\mu}\}
for tangent spacetime
T_{4}
over
M_{4}
. A displacement vector
dx^{\mu}
at point
x
of
M_{4}
forms a dual tangent basis
\{dx^{\mu}\}
for dual tangent spacetime
T_{4}^{\ast}
over
M_{4}
. These dual bases satisfy the dual condition:

\displaystyle\langle dx^{\mu},\,\partial_{\nu}\rangle=\frac{\partial x^{\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}=\eta_{\nu}^{\;\mu}.

(48)

In analogous, the gravicoordinate displacement
\delta\varkappa^{a}
and gravicoordinate derivative
\hat{\eth}_{a}
form a pair of dual field bases
\{\delta\varkappa_{a}\}
and
\{\hat{\eth}_{a}\}
. Such dual field bases meet to the following dual condition:

\displaystyle\langle\delta\varkappa^{a},\hat{\eth}_{b}\rangle\equiv\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{b}^{\;\nu}(x)\langle dx^{\mu},\partial_{\nu}\rangle=\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{b}^{\;\;\nu}(x)\eta_{\nu}^{\;\mu}=\eta_{b}^{\;\,a},

(49)

which span a pair of dual locally flat spacetimes over globally flat Minkowski spacetime
M_{4}
.

For convenience, the dual field bases
\{\hat{\eth}_{a}\}
and
\{\delta\varkappa^{a}\}
determined by the gravigauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)
as bi-covariant vector field are referred to as a pair of dual gravigauge field bases. Meanwhile, the dual locally flat field spacetimes spanned by the dual gravigauge field bases are referred to as locally flat dual
gravigauge spacetimes
, where the gravigauge spacetime spanned from gravigauge field basis
\{\hat{\eth}_{a}\}
is mentioned as tangent-like gravigauge spacetime
G_{4}
, and the dual gravigauge spacetime formed from the dual gravigauge field basis
\{\delta\varkappa^{\mathbf{A}}\}
is referred to as dual tangent-like gravigauge spacetime
G_{4}^{\ast}
. Therefore, the gravigauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)
as bi-covariant vector field is thought to be sided on the dual tangent Minkowski spacetime
T_{4}^{\ast}
and valued on the dual gravigauge spacetime
G_{4}^{\ast}
, while the dual gravigauge field
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
is defined on the gravigauge spacetime
G_{4}
and valued on the tangent Minkowski spacetime
T_{4}
. Thus
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
transforms as a bi-covariant vector field under the transformations of both spin gauge symmetry SP(1,3) and global Lorentz group symmetry SO(1,3).

It is noticed that the gravigauge field basis
\{\hat{\eth}_{a}\}
is no longer commutative, its non-commutation relation is explicitly given by:

\displaystyle[\hat{\eth}_{c}\,,\hat{\eth}_{d}]=\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\mspace{1mu}\hat{\eth}_{a},

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}=-\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{c}^{\;\mu}(x)\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{d}^{\;\nu}(x)\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\equiv\mathbf{\Omega}_{c\mspace{1mu}d}^{a}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{d\mspace{1mu}c}^{a},

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\mathbf{A}_{\nu}^{a}(x)-\partial_{\nu}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{a}(x),

(50)

which brings on a non-Abelian Lie algebra of gravicoordinate derivative operator
\hat{\eth}_{c}
. Where the structure factor
\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}
is no longer a constant and determined by the gravigauge field strenghth
\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}
. Such a non-Abelian Lie algebra leads to the emergence of non-commutative geometry in locally flat gravigauge spacetime
{\mathbf{G}}_{4}
. To explore the property of non-commutative geometry in locally flat gravigauge spacetime
{\mathbf{G}}_{4}
, it is necessary to study the dynamics of spin gravigauge field
\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}
.

In general, the gravigauge spacetime
G_{4}
and tangent Minkowski spacetime
T_{4}
bring on a
biframe spacetime

T_{4}\times G_{4}
which is associated with a dual
biframe spacetime

T_{4}^{\ast}\times G_{4}^{\ast}
over coordinate spacetime
M_{4}
. mathematically, the globally and locally flat vector spacetimes allow for a canonical identification of vectors in tangent Minkowski spacetime
T_{4}
at points with vectors in Minkowski spacetime itself
M_{4}
, and also for a canonical identification of vectors at points with its dual vectors at the same points. In physics, either tangent or dual tangent Minkowski spacetime over globally flat Minkowski spacetime is viewed as a free-motion spacetime
\mathbf{M}_{4}
. The locally flat gravigauge spacetime characterized by the gravigauge field is regarded as an
emergent spacetime

{\mathbf{G}}_{4}
.

The canonical identification for the vector spacetimes simplifies the structure of biframe spacetime to be,

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{4}=\mathbf{M}_{4}\times\bf{G}_{4},

\displaystyle\mathbf{M}_{4}\equiv T_{4}\cong T_{4}^{\ast}\cong M_{4},\quad{\mathbf{G}}_{4}\equiv G_{4}\cong G_{4}^{\ast}.

(51)

Mathematically, such a biframe spacetime structure brings about a
gravigauge fiber bundle

\mathbf{E}_{4}
, where the globally flat free-motion Minkowksi spacetime is considered as a base spacetime
\mathbf{M}_{4}
and the locally flat emergent gravigauge spacetime is viewed as a fiber
{\mathbf{G}}_{4}
. For a trivial case, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{4}\sim\mathbf{B}_{4}=\mathbf{M}_{4}\times{\mathbf{G}}_{4}.

(52)

In general, the gravigauge fiber bundle
\mathbf{E}_{4}
is related to the product biframe spacetime
\mathbf{M}_{4}\times{\mathbf{G}}_{4}
through a continuous surjective map
\Pi_{\chi}
which projects the bundle
\mathbf{E}_{4}
to the base spacetime
\mathbf{M}_{4}
. Thus the gravigauge fiber bundle
\mathbf{E}_{4}
with the surjective map
\Pi_{\chi}
is in general expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\Pi_{\chi}:\;\;\mathbf{E}_{4}\to\mathbf{M}_{4}.

(53)

Geometrically, the gravigauge fiber bundle structure of biframe spacetime is represented by
(\mathbf{E}_{4},\mathbf{M}_{4},\Pi_{\chi},{\mathbf{G}}_{4})
.

V
Scaling property of basic fields and the gauge fields and field strengths in gravigauge spacetime

It is useful to analyze the scaling property of basic fields in order to construct a scaling invariant action. Meanwhile, the introduction of biframe spacetime enables us to redefine the gauge fields and field strengths in locally flat gravigauge spacetime, which is crucial to build a gauge invariant and coordinate independent action.

V.1
Scaling property of basic fields

From the covariant derivative operator presented in Eq.(
16
), it is clear that the gauge field has the same scaling property as coordinate derivative. Under the scaling transformation of coordinates, the gauge fields transform as follows:

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\to x^{{}^{\prime}\mu}=\lambda^{-1}x^{\mu},\quad\partial_{\mu}\to\partial^{\prime}_{\mu}=\lambda\partial_{\mu},

\displaystyle\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x),\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x),A_{\mu}(x)\right)\to\left({\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x^{\prime}),\check{{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}_{\mu}(x^{\prime}),A^{\prime}_{\mu}(x^{\prime})\right)=\lambda\left({\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}(x),\check{{\mathcal{A}}}_{\mu}(x),A_{\mu}(x)\right),

(54)

with
\lambda
the constant scaling factor. Where the power of
\lambda
defines the canonical dimension of coordinates and gauge fields.

The dimensionless gravivector
\varkappa^{\mu}(x)
in gravigauge spacetime should be a scaling invariant vector field indicated from the definitions of gravicoodinator displacement
\delta\varkappa^{a}
and derivative
\hat{\eth}_{a}
operators given in Eq.(
44
). A graviscalar field
\phi(x)
is rescaled from the gravigauge field in Eq.(
45
) to provide the dimensionful gravicoodinator displacement
\delta\zeta^{a}
and derivative
\eth_{a}
operators in Eq.(
IV
). The presence of graviscalar field enables us to analyze the local and global scaling properties of basic fields:

\displaystyle\phi(x)\to\phi^{\prime}(x)=\xi(x)\phi(x),

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)\to\chi_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}\;a}(x)=\xi^{-1}(x)\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x),\quad\hat{\chi}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)\to\hat{\chi}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}\;a}(x)=\xi(x)\hat{\chi}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x),

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;b}(x)\eta_{ab}\to\chi^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}(x)=\xi^{-2}(x)\chi_{\mu\nu}(x),

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}^{\mu\nu}(x)\equiv\hat{\chi}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)\hat{\chi}_{b}^{\;\;\nu}(x)\eta^{ab}\to\hat{\chi}^{{}^{\prime}\mu\nu}(x)=\xi^{2}(x)\hat{\chi}^{\mu\nu}(x),

(55)

under the scaling gauge transformation, and

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\to x^{{}^{\prime}\mu}=\lambda^{-1}x^{\mu},\quad\phi(x)\to\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\phi(x),

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)\to\chi_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}\;a}(x^{\prime})=\lambda\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x),\quad\hat{\chi}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)\to\hat{\chi}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}\;a}(x^{\prime})=\lambda^{-1}\hat{\chi}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x),

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu\nu}(x)\to\chi^{\prime}_{\mu\nu}(x)=\lambda^{2}\chi_{\mu\nu}(x),\quad\hat{\chi}^{\mu\nu}(x)\to\hat{\chi}^{{}^{\prime}\mu\nu}(x^{\prime})=\lambda^{-2}\hat{\chi}^{\mu\nu}(x),

(56)

under the global scaling transformation.

Let us assign the powers of
\lambda
and
\xi(x)
under the global and local scaling transformations of fields to be as the global and local scaling charges
\mathcal{C}_{s}
and
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}
of fields, respectively. The canonical dimension
\mathcal{C}_{d}
of field is defined as the sum of the global and local scaling charges, i.e.:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{d}=\mathcal{C}_{s}+\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}.

(57)

It is obvious that the gauge fields
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)
as well as
A_{\mu}(x)
all have global and local scaling charges
\mathcal{C}_{s}=1
and
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}=0
with the canonical dimension
\mathcal{C}_{d}=1
, while the gravigauge field
\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)
(
\hat{\chi}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
) has global and local scaling charges
\mathcal{C}_{s}=1
(
\mathcal{C}_{s}=-1
) and
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}=-1
(
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}=1
) with the canonical dimension
\mathcal{C}_{d}=0
. A field with zero canonical dimension
\mathcal{C}_{d}=0
is referred to as
dimensionless field
and a field with zero local scaling charge
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}=0
is called as
scaling gauge invariant field
. In general, a field with both zero global and local scaling charges
\mathcal{C}_{s}=0
and
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}=0
is referred to as
scaling gauge invariant dimensionless field
.

In general, taking the dimensionless gravigauge field
\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)
and graviscalar field
\phi(x)
, we can equivalently decompose, instead of using the explicit scaling gauge invariant ones
\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
, the spin gauge field into the following two alternative parts:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)+\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)+\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x),

(58)

where the alternative spin gravigauge field
\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and spin covariant gauge field
\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
are defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{\chi}^{a\nu}F_{\mu\nu}^{b}-\hat{\chi}^{b\nu}F_{\mu\nu}^{a}-\hat{\chi}^{a\rho}\hat{\chi}^{b\sigma}F_{\rho\sigma}^{c}\chi_{\mu c}\right),

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\nu}^{\;a}(x)-\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x),

(59)

with the relations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)+\mathsf{S}_{\mu}^{ab}(x),\quad\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)-\mathsf{S}_{\mu}^{ab}(x),

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\left(\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)\hat{\chi}^{b\nu}(x)-\chi_{\mu}^{\;b}(x)\hat{\chi}^{a\nu}(x)\right)S_{\nu},

(60)

and the scaling invariant gravigauge field strength,

\displaystyle\mathrm{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\equiv d_{\mu}\chi_{\nu}^{\;a}(x)-d_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)\equiv F_{\mu\nu}^{a}+S_{\mu\nu}^{a},

\displaystyle S_{\mu\nu}^{a}\equiv S_{\mu}\chi_{\nu}^{\;a}(x)-S_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x),

\displaystyle d_{\nu}\equiv\partial_{\nu}+S_{\nu},\quad S_{\nu}\equiv\partial_{\nu}\ln\phi(x),

(61)

which indicates that
\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and
\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
become no longer scaling gauge invariance. Under the scaling gauge transformation, they transform explicitly as follows :

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\to\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(x)=\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)+\left(\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)\hat{\chi}^{b\nu}(x)-\chi_{\mu}^{\;b}(x)\hat{\chi}^{a\nu}(x)\right)\partial_{\nu}\ln\xi(x),

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\to\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{{}^{\prime}ab}(x)=\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)-\left(\chi_{\mu}^{\;a}(x)\hat{\chi}^{b\nu}(x)-\chi_{\mu}^{\;b}(x)\hat{\chi}^{a\nu}(x)\right)\partial_{\nu}\ln\xi(x)

(62)

Correspondingly, the spin gauge field strength is decomposed into the following two alternative parts:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\equiv R_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)+\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)+{\mathbf{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x),

(63)

with the definitions:

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\mathsf{\Omega}_{\nu}^{ab}-\partial_{\nu}\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{ab}+\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu c}^{a}\mathsf{\Omega}_{\nu}^{cb}-\mathsf{\Omega}_{\nu c}^{a}\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu}^{cb},

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)=\mathsf{D}_{\mu}\mathsf{A}_{\nu}^{ab}-\mathsf{D}_{\nu}\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{ab}+\mathsf{A}_{\mu c}^{a}\mathsf{A}_{\nu}^{cb}-\mathsf{A}_{\nu c}^{a}\mathsf{A}_{\mu}^{cb},

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}_{\mu}\mathsf{A}_{\nu}^{ab}(x)=\partial_{\mu}\mathsf{A}_{\nu}^{ab}+\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu c}^{a}\mathsf{A}_{\nu}^{cb}+\mathsf{\Omega}_{\mu c}^{b}\mathsf{A}_{\nu}^{ac},

(64)

and the relations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\equiv R_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)+\mathsf{S}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x),\quad{\mathbf{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)-\mathsf{S}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x),

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\mu\nu}^{ab}(x)=\mathsf{D}_{\mu}\mathsf{S}_{\nu}^{ab}-\mathsf{D}_{\nu}\mathsf{S}_{\mu}^{ab}+\mathsf{S}_{\mu c}^{a}\mathsf{S}_{\nu}^{cb}-\mathsf{S}_{\nu c}^{a}\mathsf{S}_{\mu}^{cb},

(65)

V.2
Gauge fields and field strengths in locally flat gravigauge spacetime

The gravitational origin of spin gauge symmetry indicates that the scaling gauge invariant gravigauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}
(
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\,\mu}
) as bi-covariant vector field in biframe spacetime can be regarded as Goldstone-like boson, which enables us to project the spin gauge field
{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)
and electromagnetic gauge field
A_{\mu}(x)
as vector fields in free-motion Minkowski spacetime
\mathbf{M}_{4}
into the vector fields in locally flat gravigauge spacetime
{\mathbf{G}}_{4}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}^{ab}\equiv\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{c}^{\;\,\mu}(x){\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x)\equiv\phi^{-1}{\mathcal{A}}_{c}^{ab},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{A}_{c}\equiv\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{c}^{\;\,\mu}(x)A_{\mu}(x)\equiv\phi^{-1}A_{c},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{c}^{ab}\equiv\hat{\chi}_{c}^{\;\,\mu}(x){\mathcal{A}}_{\mu}^{ab}(x),\quad A_{c}\equiv\hat{\chi}_{c}^{\;\,\mu}(x)A_{\mu}(x),

(66)

where
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}^{ab}
and
\boldsymbol{A}_{c}
appear as scaling gauge invariant dimensionless fields, and
{\mathcal{A}}_{c}^{ab}
and
A_{c}
become local scaling charged gauge fields. The scaling gauge invariant dimensionless covariant derivative operator in locally flat gravigauge spacetime is defined as:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{D}}}_{c}\equiv\hat{\eth}_{c}-i\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}\equiv\hat{\eth}_{c}-i\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}^{ab}\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{ab}\equiv\phi^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}_{c},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}_{c}\equiv\eth_{c}-i{\mathcal{A}}_{c}\equiv\eth_{c}-i{\mathcal{A}}_{c}^{ab}\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{ab},

(67)

for the spin gauge field, and

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{c}\equiv\hat{\eth}_{c}-i\boldsymbol{A}_{c}\equiv\phi^{-1}D_{c},

\displaystyle D_{c}\equiv\eth_{c}-iA_{c},

(68)

for the electromagnetic gauge field. Where
\hat{\eth}_{c}\equiv\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{c}^{\;\,\mu}\partial_{\mu}
is the scaling gauge invariant dimensionless gravicoordinate derivative operator given in Eq.(
44
) and
\eth_{c}\equiv\hat{\chi}_{c}^{\;\,\mu}\partial_{\mu}
is the local scaling charged one. The commutator of the covariant derivative operator is given by

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{D}}}_{c},\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{D}}}_{d}]=\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{D}}}_{a}-i\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd},\quad[\boldsymbol{D}_{c},\boldsymbol{D}_{d}]=\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\boldsymbol{D}_{a}-i\boldsymbol{F}_{cd},

\displaystyle[{\mathcal{D}}_{c},{\mathcal{D}}_{d}]=\mathsf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}{\mathcal{D}}_{a}-i{\mathcal{F}}_{cd},\quad[D_{c},D_{d}]=\mathsf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}D_{a}-iF_{cd},

(69)

which define the scaling gauge invariant dimensionless gauge field strengths in locally flat gravigauge spacetime:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}=-\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{c}^{\;\mu}(x)\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{d}^{\;\nu}(x)\mathsf{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\equiv-\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{cd}^{a},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd}=\hat{\eth}_{c}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{d}-\hat{\eth}_{d}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}+[\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c},\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{d}]-\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{a}\equiv\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd}^{\;ab}\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{ab},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}_{cd}=\hat{\eth}_{c}\boldsymbol{A}_{d}-\hat{\eth}_{d}\boldsymbol{A}_{c}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\boldsymbol{A}_{a},

(70)

and the corresponding local scaling charged gauge field strengths:

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\equiv-F_{cd}^{a}\equiv-\hat{\chi}_{c}^{\;\mu}(x)\hat{\chi}_{d}^{\;\nu}(x)F_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}\equiv{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab}\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{ab}=\eth_{c}{\mathcal{A}}_{d}-\eth_{d}{\mathcal{A}}_{c}+[{\mathcal{A}}_{c},{\mathcal{A}}_{d}]-\mathsf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}{\mathcal{A}}_{a}\equiv\phi^{2}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd},

\displaystyle F_{cd}=\eth_{c}A_{d}-\eth_{d}A_{c}-\mathsf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}A_{a}\equiv\phi^{2}\boldsymbol{F}_{cd},

\displaystyle\mathrm{F}_{cd}^{a}\equiv\hat{\chi}_{c}^{\;\mu}(x)\hat{\chi}_{d}^{\;\nu}(x)\mathrm{F}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\equiv F_{cd}^{a}+S_{cd}^{a}\equiv\phi\,\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{cd}^{a},

\displaystyle S_{cd}^{a}\equiv\hat{\chi}_{c}^{\;\mu}(x)\hat{\chi}_{d}^{\;\nu}(x)S_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)=(S_{c}\,\eta_{d}^{\;a}-S_{d}\,\eta_{c}^{\;a}),\quad S_{c}\equiv\eth_{c}\ln\phi.

(71)

The spin gauge and scaling gauge invariant gravigauge field strength is defined as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd}^{a}\equiv\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{c}^{\;\mu}(x)\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{d}^{\;\nu}(x){\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)\equiv\phi^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{a}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{a}\equiv{\mathbf{F}}_{cd}^{a}+S_{cd}^{a},\quad{\mathbf{F}}_{cd}^{a}\equiv\hat{\chi}_{c}^{\;\mu}\hat{\chi}_{d}^{\;\nu}{\mathbf{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}\equiv\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\nu}^{a}(x)-\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}^{a}(x)+{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu\,b}^{a}(x)\chi_{\nu}^{b}(x)-{\mathcal{A}}_{\nu\,b}^{a}(x)\chi_{\mu}^{b}(x),

(72)

with
{\mathcal{F}}_{\mu\nu}^{a}(x)
being the spin gauge and scaling gauge invariant gravigauge field strength defined in Eq.(
III.1
).

The field strengths
{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab}
and
F_{cd}
possess the local scaling charge
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}=2
, while
{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{a}
and
\mathsf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\equiv F_{cd}^{a}
have the local scaling charge
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}=1
as indicated from their relations to the scaling gauge invariant dimensionless gauge field strengths. Where
\mathbf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\equiv\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{cd}^{\;a}
(
\mathsf{\Omega}_{[cd]}^{a}\equiv F_{cd}^{\;a}
) brings on the gravigauge field strength arising from the non-commutation relation of the gravicoordinate derivative operator.
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd}^{\;ab}
(
{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab}
) defines the field strength of spin gauge field and
\boldsymbol{F}_{cd}
(
F_{cd}
) represents the field strength of electromagnetic gauge field.

The gravitational origin of spin gauge symmetry brings the scaling gauge invariant dimensionless spin gauge field
\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}^{ab}
into the two parts:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}^{ab}=\mathbf{\Omega}_{c}^{ab}+\mathbf{A}_{c}^{ab}\equiv\phi^{-1}{\mathcal{A}}_{c}^{ab}\equiv\phi^{-1}(\mathsf{\Omega}_{c}^{ab}+\mathsf{A}_{c}^{ab}),

(73)

where
\mathbf{\Omega}_{c}^{ab}
and
\mathbf{A}_{c}^{ab}
correspond to the scaling gauge invariant dimensionless spin gravigauge field and spin covariant-gauge field in locally flat gravigauge spacetime.
\mathbf{\Omega}_{c}^{ab}
characterizes the gravitational origin of gauge symmetry determined by the dual gravigauge fields
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\,a}
and
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\,\mu}
with the following explicit form:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Omega}_{c}^{ab}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\,\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{a\mu}\hat{\eth}_{c}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\,b}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{b\mu}\hat{\eth}_{c}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\,a}-\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{c}^{\;\,\mu}(\hat{\eth}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\,b}-\hat{\eth}^{b}\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\,a})

(74)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(\hat{\eth}^{a}\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{b\mu}-\hat{\eth}^{b}\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{a\mu})\mathbf{A}_{\mu c}]\equiv\eta^{ad}\eta^{bd^{\prime}}\mathbf{\Omega}_{cdd^{\prime}}.

The scaling gauge invariant dimensionless field strength of spin gauge field is correspondingly expressed into the following two parts:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd}^{\;ab}=\mathbf{R}_{cd}^{\;ab}+{\mathbf{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab}\equiv\phi^{-2}\left(\mathsf{R}_{cd}^{\;ab}+\mathsf{F}_{cd}^{\;ab}\right),

(75)

with the explicit forms:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd}^{\;ab}=\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{c}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{d}^{ab}-\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{d}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}^{\;ab}+(\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{cb^{\prime}}^{a}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{d}^{b^{\prime}b}-\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{db^{\prime}}^{a}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c}^{b^{\prime}b})\equiv\phi^{-2}{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab},

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{cd}^{\;ab}=\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{c}\mathbf{\Omega}_{d}^{ab}-\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{d}\mathbf{\Omega}_{c}^{ab}+\mathbf{\Omega}_{cb^{\prime}}^{a}\mathbf{\Omega}_{d}^{b^{\prime}b}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{db^{\prime}}^{a}\mathbf{\Omega}_{c}^{b^{\prime}b}\equiv\phi^{-2}\left(\mathsf{R}_{cd}^{\;ab}+\mathsf{S}_{cd}^{\;ab}\right),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab}=\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{c}\mathbf{A}_{d}^{ab}-\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{d}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{ab}+\mathbf{A}_{cb^{\prime}}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{d}^{b^{\prime}b}-\mathbf{A}_{db^{\prime}}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{b^{\prime}b}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\mathbf{\Omega}_{cb^{\prime}}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{d}^{b^{\prime}b}+\mathbf{\Omega}_{cb^{\prime}}^{b}\mathbf{A}_{d}^{ab^{\prime}}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{db^{\prime}}^{a}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{b^{\prime}b}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{db^{\prime}}^{b}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{ab^{\prime}}\equiv\phi^{-2}\left(\mathsf{F}_{cd}^{\;ab}-\mathsf{S}_{cd}^{\;ab}\right),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{c}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{d}^{ab}=\hat{\eth}_{c}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{d}^{ab}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{cd}^{c^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{c^{\prime}}^{ab},\quad\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{c}\mathbf{A}_{d}^{ab}=\hat{\eth}_{c}\mathbf{A}_{d}^{ab}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{cd}^{c^{\prime}}\mathbf{A}_{c^{\prime}}^{ab},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{c}\mathbf{\Omega}_{d}^{ab}=\hat{\eth}_{c}\mathbf{\Omega}_{d}^{ab}-\mathbf{\Omega}_{cd}^{c^{\prime}}\mathbf{\Omega}_{c^{\prime}}^{ab},

(76)

where
\mathbf{R}_{cd}^{\;ab}
and
{\mathbf{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab}
correspond to the scaling gauge invariant dimensionless spin gravigauge field strength and spin covariant-gauge field strength.
\boldsymbol{{\check{{\mathcal{D}}}}}_{c}
is regarded as the scaling gauge invariant dimensionless covariant derivative of gravicoordinate derivative
\hat{\eth}_{c}
under the presence of spin gravigauge field in locally flat gravigauge spacetime. It can be checked that
\mathsf{R}_{cd}^{\;ab}
and
\mathsf{F}_{cd}^{\;ab}
with corresponding derivatives
\check{{\mathcal{D}}}_{c}
and
\eth_{c}
get the similar definitions in light of the scaling charged quantities.

VI
Gauge and scaling invariant action and gauge-gravity-geometry correspondence in the framework of gravitational quantum field theory

With the above discussions and analyses, we are able to construct the gauge and scaling invariant action in locally flat gravigauge spacetime and biframe spacetime. Meanwhile, we are going to present a general demonstration on the gauge-gravity and gravity-geometry correspondences within the framework of gravitational quantum field theory. We shall begin with discussing the scaling gauge fixing and the Einstein basis.

VI.1
Scaling gauge fixing and Einstein basis

The gravigauge field expressed in Eq.(
45
) indicates a hidden scaling gauge invariance, which allows us to take an essential scaling gauge transformation
\xi_{e}(x)
to set a special gauge fixing condition, so that the graviscalar field
\phi(x)
can be transformed into a constant scale
M_{\kappa}
:

\displaystyle\phi(x)\to\phi^{(e)}(x)=\xi_{e}(x)\phi(x)\equiv M_{\kappa}.

(77)

The dimensionless gravigauge field
\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)
gets a corresponding scaling gauge transformation in order to preserve the scaling gauge invariance of gravigauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)
, i.e.:

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)\to\chi_{\mu}^{(e)\mspace{1mu}a}(x)\equiv\xi_{e}^{-1}(x)\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x).

(78)

In such a special scaling gauge transformation, the scaling gauge invariant gravigauge field and gravimetric field can be written into the following forms:

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\phi(x)\chi_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)\to\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{(e)\mspace{1mu}a}(x)=M_{\kappa}\chi_{\mu}^{(e)\mspace{1mu}a}(x),

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{\mu\nu}(x)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\phi^{2}(x)\chi_{\mu\nu}(x)\to\mathsf{H}_{\mu\nu}^{(e)}(x)=M_{\kappa}^{2}\chi^{(e)}_{\mu\nu}(x),

(79)

which fixes the scaling gauge symmetry into the so-called Einstein basis. Where the constant mass
M_{\kappa}
plays a role as a
fundamental mass scale
.
\chi_{\mu}^{(e)a}(x)
and
\chi_{\mu\nu}^{(e)}(x)
are mentioned to be the dimensionless gravigauge field and gravimetric field in Einstein basis.

VI.2
Gauge and scaling invariant action in locally flat gravigauge spacetime

We are now in the position to construct the gauge and scaling invariant action for the chirality-based Dirac spinor field appearing in the SM. It is believed that the SM is an effective theory as it involves 18 unknown parameters, so the action that we are going to build should also be an effective action. For our present purpose, we just provide an action with keeping the lowest order terms by following along the gauge and scaling invariance principle. The explicit form for such an action in locally flat gravigauge spacetime is found to have the following simple form:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{D}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int[\delta^{4}\varkappa]\,\mathfrak{L}\equiv\int[\delta^{4}\varkappa]\;\{(\bar{\Psi}_{-}\Sigma_{-}^{a}i\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{D}}}_{a}\Psi_{-}-\frac{m}{M_{\kappa}}\bar{\Psi}_{-}\Sigma_{-}^{6}\Psi_{-}+H.c.)

(80)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}g_{E}^{-2}\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta^{dd^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{F}_{cd}\boldsymbol{F}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{4}g_{G}^{-2}\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta^{dd^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd}^{\;ab}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}ab}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\bar{\eta}^{cdc^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\frac{m_{G}^{2}}{M_{\kappa}^{2}}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{cd}^{a}\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{F}}}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{4}g_{G}^{-2}\tilde{\eta}^{cd\mspace{1mu}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{cd}^{a}\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}\},

with the total covariant derivative,

\displaystyle i\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{D}}}_{a}\equiv i\hat{\eth}_{a}+\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{a}+\boldsymbol{A}_{a}\equiv i\hat{\eth}_{a}+\boldsymbol{{\mathcal{A}}}_{a}^{bc}\frac{1}{2}\Sigma_{bc}+\boldsymbol{A}_{a}.

(81)

It is noted that the gravigauge field
\mathbf{A}_{\mu}^{\;\;a}(x)
does not couple to the spinor field via the usual covariant derivative due to the chirality property of the group generators
\Sigma_{-}^{a}\Sigma_{-}^{b}=0
, it couples alternatively to the spinor field through the gravicoordinate derivative
\Sigma_{-}^{a}\hat{\eth}_{a}\equiv\Sigma_{-}^{a}\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}\partial_{\mu}\equiv\hat{\mathsf{H}}^{\mu\nu}\mathbf{A}_{\mu a}\Sigma_{-}^{a}\partial_{\nu}
in locally flat gravigauge spacetime. In fact, the kinematics of all basic fields acts as the source of gravitational interaction along with the gravicoordinate derivative characterized by the dual gravigauge field
\hat{\mathbf{A}}_{a}^{\;\;\mu}(x)
, which actually brings on the comprehension why all motional fields get universal gravitational interactions.

It is noticed that all basic fields in the action appear as scaling gauge invariant dimensionless fields. Namely, all basic fields and operators in the above action have zero global and local scaling charges
\mathcal{C}_{s}=0
and
\check{\mathcal{C}}_{s}=0
. The mass terms are also rescaled into dimensionless quantities in light of the fundamental mass scale, i.e.,
m/M_{\kappa}
and
m_{G}/M_{\kappa}
.

The tensor
\bar{\eta}^{cd\mspace{1mu}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}
is given by the following general structure:

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}^{cd\mspace{1mu}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\equiv\alpha_{G}\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta^{dd^{\prime}}\eta_{aa^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{W}(\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta_{a^{\prime}}^{d}\eta_{a}^{d^{\prime}}+\eta^{dd^{\prime}}\eta_{a^{\prime}}^{c}\eta_{a}^{c^{\prime}})

(82)

with
\alpha_{G}
and
\alpha_{W}
being two coupling constants, and the tensor structure
\tilde{\eta}^{cdc^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\eta}^{cdc^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta^{dd^{\prime}}\eta_{aa^{\prime}}+\eta^{cc^{\prime}}(\eta_{a^{\prime}}^{d}\eta_{a}^{d^{\prime}}-2\eta_{a}^{d}\eta_{a^{\prime}}^{d^{\prime}})+\eta^{dd^{\prime}}(\eta_{a^{\prime}}^{c}\eta_{a}^{c^{\prime}}-2\eta_{a}^{c}\eta_{a^{\prime}}^{c^{\prime}}),

(83)

which preserves the spin gauge invariance for gravitational interaction of gravigauge field. It can be verified explicitly from the following relation and identity:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\eta}^{cd\mspace{1mu}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\mathfrak{A}\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{cd}^{a}\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}=\eta_{a}^{\;d}\eta_{b}^{\;c}\mathfrak{A}\mathbf{R}_{cd}^{\;ab}+2\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{A}\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{a\mu}\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{ca}^{c})

\displaystyle\qquad\equiv\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\eta}^{cd\mspace{1mu}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\chi\phi^{2}\mathrm{F}_{cd}^{a}\mathrm{F}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{4}\tilde{\eta}^{cd\mspace{1mu}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\chi\phi^{2}(F_{cd}^{a}+S_{cd}^{a})(F_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}+S_{cd}^{a})

\displaystyle\qquad=\eta_{a}^{\;d}\eta_{b}^{\;c}\chi\phi^{2}(R_{cd}^{\;ab}+\mathsf{S}_{cd}^{\;ab})+2\partial_{\mu}(\chi\phi^{2}\hat{\chi}^{a\mu}\mathrm{F}_{ca}^{c}),

(84)

where the term
\partial_{\mu}(\mathfrak{A}\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{a\mu}\boldsymbol{{\mathsf{F}}}_{ca}^{c})
(or
\partial_{\mu}(\chi\phi^{2}\hat{\chi}^{a\mu}\mathrm{F}_{ca}^{c})
) on the right-hand side of equality appears as a total derivative in the action.

From the above identity and relation, the above spin gauge invariant action via a hidden scaling gauge formalism (Eq.(
80
)) can be rewritten into the following spin gauge and scaling gauge invariant action:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{D}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int[\delta^{4}\zeta]\,{\mathcal{L}}\equiv\int[\delta^{4}\zeta]\;\{(\bar{\varPsi}_{-}\Sigma_{-}^{a}i{\mathcal{D}}_{a}\varPsi_{-}-\frac{m}{M_{\kappa}}\phi\,\bar{\varPsi}_{-}\Sigma_{-}^{6}\varPsi_{-}+H.c.)

(85)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}g_{E}^{-2}\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta^{dd^{\prime}}F_{cd}F_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{4}g_{G}^{-2}\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta^{dd^{\prime}}{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab}{\mathcal{F}}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}ab}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\bar{\eta}^{cdc^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\frac{m_{G}^{2}}{M_{\kappa}^{2}}\phi^{2}{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{a}{\mathcal{F}}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}+\frac{1}{4}g_{G}^{-2}\tilde{\eta}^{cd\mspace{1mu}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\phi^{2}\mathrm{F}_{cd}^{a}\mathrm{F}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}\},

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int[\delta^{4}\zeta]\mspace{1mu}\{(\bar{\varPsi}_{-}\Sigma_{-}^{a}i{\mathcal{D}}_{a}\varPsi_{-}-\frac{m}{M_{\kappa}}\phi\,\bar{\varPsi}_{-}\Sigma_{-}^{6}\varPsi_{-}+H.c.)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta^{dd^{\prime}}F_{cd}F_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{4}\eta^{cc^{\prime}}\eta^{dd^{\prime}}{\mathcal{F}}_{cd}^{\;ab}{\mathcal{F}}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}ab}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\bar{\eta}^{cdc^{\prime}d^{\prime}}_{aa^{\prime}}\frac{m_{G}^{2}}{M_{\kappa}^{2}}\phi^{2}({\mathbf{F}}_{cd}^{a}+S_{cd}^{a})({\mathbf{F}}_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}}+S_{c^{\prime}d^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}})

\displaystyle+
